Riemannian Newton Methods for Energy Minimization Problems of Kohn–Sham Type
Authors: R. Altmann, D. Peterseim, T. Stykel
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
This paper is devoted to the numerical solution of constrained energy minimization problems arising in computational physics and chemistry such as the Gross–Pitaevskii and Kohn–Sham models. In particular, we introduce Riemannian Newton methods on the infinite-dimensional Stiefel and Grassmann manifolds. We study the geometry of these two manifolds, its impact on the Newton algorithms, and present expressions of the Riemannian Hessians in the infinite-dimensional setting, which are suitable for variational spatial discretizations. A series of numerical experiments illustrates the performance of the methods and demonstrates their supremacy compared to other well-established schemes such as the self-consistent field iteration and gradient descent schemes.

Introduction

The Kohn–Sham model [31, 37, 38] is a prototypical example of a constrained energy minimization problem stated on the infinite-dimensional Stiefel manifold. This means that the sought-after minimizer is a p-frame of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-orthonormal functions. Another well-known example is the Gross–Pitaevskii model for Bose–Einstein condensates of ultracold bosonic gases [39, 43]. Here, the special case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=1$$\end{document}p=1 is of interest, where we seek a single (minimizing) function on the unit sphere in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2, representing a unit mass constraint. Since these two applications are relevant for different communities, numerical methods are mostly considered separately. One aim of this paper is to give a unified approach to solving energy minimization problems. More precisely, we introduce Riemannian Newton methods for minimizing energy functionals of Kohn–Sham type, which also includes the Gross–Pitaevskii model. In general, the here considered PDE problems require a special treatment in terms of sparsity and dimension-independent methods, which is not part of existing general purpose optimization packages.

The numerical solution of the Gross–Pitaevskii model has been studied extensively in recent years. The most common numerical techniques are iterative methods based on Riemannian (conjugate) gradient descent methods or discretized Riemannian gradient flows in various metrics [10, 11, 20, 22, 26, 29, 36, 44, 53]. A conceptually different approach is the J-method [5, 33] with its inimitable sensitivity with regard to spectral shifts, allowing remarkable speed-ups in a Rayleigh quotient iteration manner. Reformulating the minimization problem as an eigenvalue problem with eigenvector nonlinearity—also known as nonlinear eigenvector problem—the self-consistent field iteration (SCF) can be employed; see [14, 23, 45]. This method involves the solution of a linear eigenvalue problem in each step and is strongly connected to the Newton method [28, 34]. Considering the extended nonlinear system including the normalization constraint also allows a direct application of Newton or Newton-type methods [12, 13, 24]. For an extended review on numerical methods for the Gross–Pitaevskii model, we refer to [28].

Most of the above approaches (with appropriate adjustments) have been applied to the Kohn–Sham model as well. This includes the direct constrained minimization algorithm [3, 47, 52] and the energy-adaptive gradient descent method [8]—both based on Riemannian optimization—as well as gradient flow schemes [21, 32]. Moreover, the SCF algorithm with different types of mixing is very popular in the computational chemistry community; see, e.g., [9, 15, 18, 19, 40]. For a discretized and simplified Kohn–Sham model (without the external potential and the exchange-correlation energy), global convergence and local second-order convergence of an inexact Riemannian Newton method on the Grassmann manifold has been shown in [54]. An overview of existing software packages for density functional theory problems can be found in [35].

In this paper, the point of origin is an energy functional defined on the infinite-dimensional Stiefel manifold, which we introduce in Sect. 2. For a better understanding, we recall definitions and properties of the Stiefel manifold and corresponding retractions in Sect. 3. Moreover, we provide formulae for the Riemannian gradient and the Riemannian Hessian which are needed for the Newton iteration. Since the considered energy functional is invariant under orthogonal matrices, we also discuss the infinite-dimensional Grassmann manifold and examine a connection of its tangent space to a certain subspace of the tangent space of the Stiefel manifold. The resulting Newton algorithms are then subject of Sect. 4. In particular, we present an inexact Riemannian Newton method on the Grassmann manifold. In Sect. 5, we consider the two mentioned examples of the Gross–Pitaevskii and the Kohn–Sham model in more detail. For both applications, we derive the formulae including a spatial discretization and illustrate the supremacy of the inexact Newton approach compared to well-established methods such as the SCF iteration and gradient descent schemes.

Energy and Applications

For a p-frame \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\phi }}\in V$$\end{document}ϕ∈V, we consider the energy functional3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {E}}({\varvec{\phi }})&= \frac{1}{2}\, \int \limits _{\Omega } {{\,\text {tr}\,}}\bigl ((\nabla {\varvec{\phi }}(x))^T\nabla {\varvec{\phi }}(x)\bigr ) \,\text{ d }x+ \int \limits _{\Omega } \vartheta (x)\, \rho ({\varvec{\phi }}(x)) \,\text{ d }x + \frac{1}{2}\, \int \limits _{\Omega } \varGamma (\rho ({\varvec{\phi }}(x))) \,\text{ d }x \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E(ϕ)=12∫Ωtr((∇ϕ(x))T∇ϕ(x))dx+∫Ωϑ(x)ρ(ϕ(x))dx+12∫ΩΓ(ρ(ϕ(x)))dxwith an external potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta $$\end{document}ϑ, the density function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ({\varvec{\phi }}) = {\varvec{\phi }}\cdot {\varvec{\phi }}$$\end{document}ρ(ϕ)=ϕ·ϕ, and a smooth nonlinearity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varGamma (\rho )$$\end{document}Γ(ρ). Our aim is to minimize this energy functional on the infinite-dimensional Stiefel manifold of index p given by4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {St}(p,V)= \big \{ {\varvec{\phi }}\in V:\llbracket {{\varvec{\phi }}},{{\varvec{\phi }}}\rrbracket _H = I_p \big \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}St(p,V)={ϕ∈V:〚ϕ,ϕ〛H=Ip}.In other words, we are interested in solving the constrained minimization problem5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \min _{{\varvec{\phi }}\in \text {St}(p,V)} {\mathcal {E}}({\varvec{\phi }}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}minϕ∈St(p,V)E(ϕ).A state of lowest energy is called the ground state. Such states play an important role in quantum-mechanical models as they represent a most stable configuration of atoms and molecules. These models include two famous applications in computational physics and chemistry.

Example 1

(Gross–Pitaevskii model) For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=1$$\end{document}p=1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varGamma (\rho ) = \frac{1}{2} \kappa \rho ^2$$\end{document}Γ(ρ)=12κρ2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}κ∈R, the energy functional takes the form6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {E}}_{\textrm{GP}}(\phi ) = \frac{1}{2}\, \int \limits _{\Omega } \Vert \nabla \phi (x)\Vert ^2 \,\text {d}x+ \int \limits _{\Omega } \vartheta (x)\, \phi (x)^2 \,\text {d}x+ \frac{\kappa }{4}\, \int \limits _{\Omega } \phi (x)^4 \,\text {d}x. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}EGP(ϕ)=12∫Ω‖∇ϕ(x)‖2dx+∫Ωϑ(x)ϕ(x)2dx+κ4∫Ωϕ(x)4dx.This is the well-known Gross–Pitaevskii energy used in the modeling of Bose–Einstein condensates of ultracold bosonic gases [39, 43]. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta \in L^\infty (\Omega )$$\end{document}ϑ∈L∞(Ω) is the magnetic trapping potential, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi \in H_0^1(\Omega )$$\end{document}ϕ∈H01(Ω) is the quantum state of the Bose–Einstein condensate, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ characterizes the strength and the direction of particle interactions.

Example 2

(Kohn–Sham model) The (non-local) nonlinearity\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\phi }}$$\end{document}ϕ denotes a wave function with p components called single-particle orbitals and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ({\varvec{\phi }})$$\end{document}ρ(ϕ) is the electronic charge density. Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _\text {ion}$$\end{document}ϑion is the ionic potential, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _\text {xc}(\rho )$$\end{document}ϵxc(ρ) is the exchange-correlation energy per particle in a homogeneous electron gas of density \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho $$\end{document}ρ. This model is based on the so-called density functional theory [31], which allows a significant reduction of the degrees of freedom [17, 37, 38]. The last integral in (7) is a local density approximation to the exchange-correlation energy obtained by using semi-empirically knowledge of the model [42]. In the Kohn–Sham model, a ground state corresponds to the low-energy wave function of the considered molecule and the orthogonality condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\llbracket {{\varvec{\phi }}},{{\varvec{\phi }}}\rrbracket _H = I_p$$\end{document}〚ϕ,ϕ〛H=Ip means that there is no interaction between the electrons in different orbitals.

At this point, it should be emphasized that, since the energy functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {E}}$$\end{document}E in (3) is invariant under orthogonal transformations, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {E}}({\varvec{\phi }})={\mathcal {E}}({\varvec{\phi }}Q)$$\end{document}E(ϕ)=E(ϕQ) for all orthogonal matrices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q\in {\mathbb {R}}^{p\times p}$$\end{document}Q∈Rp×p, the optimal solution to the minimization problem (5) is not unique. To overcome this difficulty, we will transfer this problem to the infinite-dimensional Grassmann manifold defined in Sect. 3.2.

Connection to Nonlinear Eigenvector Problems

Remark 1

Due to the symmetry of the bilinear form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\tilde{a}}}_{\varvec{\phi }}$$\end{document}a~ϕ, we conclude that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big ( \phi _i, ({\mathcal {J}}{\mathcal {A}}_{\varvec{\phi }}\,{\varvec{\phi }})_j \big )_{L^2(\Omega )} = {{\tilde{a}}}_{{\varvec{\phi }}}(\phi _i, \phi _j) = {{\tilde{a}}}_{{\varvec{\phi }}}(\phi _j, \phi _i) = \big ( \phi _j, ({\mathcal {J}}{\mathcal {A}}_{\varvec{\phi }}\,{\varvec{\phi }})_i \big )_{L^2(\Omega )} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(ϕi,(JAϕϕ)j)L2(Ω)=a~ϕ(ϕi,ϕj)=a~ϕ(ϕj,ϕi)=(ϕj,(JAϕϕ)i)L2(Ω)for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i,j=1,\ldots ,p$$\end{document}i,j=1,…,p. This means that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\llbracket {{\varvec{\phi }}},{{\mathcal {J}}{\mathcal {A}}_{\varvec{\phi }}\,{\varvec{\phi }}}\rrbracket _H$$\end{document}〚ϕ,JAϕϕ〛H is symmetric for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\phi }}\in V$$\end{document}ϕ∈V.

The Infinite-Dimensional Stiefel and Grassmann Manifolds

In this section, we summarize definitions and properties of the infinite-dimensional Stiefel and Grassmann manifolds and their tangent spaces, which lay the foundation of the Riemannian optimization schemes in the upcoming section.

The Stiefel Manifold

We consider the infinite-dimensional Stiefel manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}St(p,V) defined in (4). It is an embedded submanifold of the Hilbert space V and has co-dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\,(p+1)/2$$\end{document}p(p+1)/2; see [8]. The tangent space of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}St(p,V) at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\phi }}\in \text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}ϕ∈St(p,V) is given by14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T_{\varvec{\phi }}\,\text {St}(p,V)= \big \{ {\varvec{\eta }}\in V:\llbracket {{\varvec{\eta }}},{{\varvec{\phi }}}\rrbracket _H + \llbracket {{\varvec{\phi }}},{{\varvec{\eta }}}\rrbracket _H = 0_p \big \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TϕSt(p,V)={η∈V:〚η,ϕ〛H+〚ϕ,η〛H=0p}.The Riemannian structure of the Stiefel manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}St(p,V) strongly depends on an underlying metric. Within this paper, we equip \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} For this purpose, we can use retractions defined as follows. first-order approximations to the exponential mapping on a Riemannian manifold and are often much easier to compute. In [8], several retractions on the Stiefel manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}St(p,V) have been introduced. They can be considered as an extension of the corresponding concepts on the matrix Stiefel manifold (see, e.g., [2, 46]) to the infinite-dimensional case.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{v}\in V$$\end{document}v∈V with linearly independent components, we consider the qR decomposition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{v}=\varvec{q}R$$\end{document}v=qR, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{q}\in \text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}q∈St(p,V) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R\in {\mathbb {R}}^{p\times p}$$\end{document}R∈Rp×p is upper triangular. Such a decomposition exists and is unique if we additionally require that R has positive diagonal elements. Then the qR decomposition based retraction is defined as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}^{qR}({\varvec{\phi }},{\varvec{\eta }})={{\,\textrm{qf}\,}}({\varvec{\phi }}+{\varvec{\eta }})$$\end{document}RqR(ϕ,η)=qf(ϕ+η), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{qf}\,}}({\varvec{\phi }}+{\varvec{\eta }})$$\end{document}qf(ϕ+η) denotes the factor from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}St(p,V) in the qR decomposition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\phi }}+{\varvec{\eta }}$$\end{document}ϕ+η. Such a factor can be computed, e.g., by the modified Gram-Schmidt orthonormalization procedure on V presented in [8].

An alternative retraction can be defined by using the polar decomposition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{v}=\varvec{u}S$$\end{document}v=uS, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{u}\in \text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}u∈St(p,V) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\in {\mathbb {R}}^{p\times p}$$\end{document}S∈Rp×p is symmetric and positive definite. Assuming that the components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{v}$$\end{document}v are linearly independent, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S=\llbracket {\varvec{v}},{\varvec{v}}\rrbracket _H^{1/2}$$\end{document}S=〚v,v〛H1/2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{u}=\varvec{v}\,\llbracket {\varvec{v}},{\varvec{v}}\rrbracket _H^{-1/2}$$\end{document}u=v〚v,v〛H-1/2 are uniquely defined. This leads to the polar decomposition based retraction\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {R}}^\textrm{pol}({\varvec{\phi }},{\varvec{\eta }}) = ({\varvec{\phi }}+{\varvec{\eta }})\llbracket {{\varvec{\phi }}+{\varvec{\eta }}},{{\varvec{\phi }}+{\varvec{\eta }}}\rrbracket _H^{-1/2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Rpol(ϕ,η)=(ϕ+η)〚ϕ+η,ϕ+η〛H-1/2,which is of second-order. Indeed, computing the second-order derivative of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {R}}^\textrm{pol}_{\varvec{\phi }}(t{\varvec{\eta }})$$\end{document}Rϕpol(tη) at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=0$$\end{document}t=0 and exploiting (16), we obtain that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\textrm{d}^2}{\textrm{d}t^2} {\mathcal {R}}^\textrm{pol}_{\varvec{\phi }}(t{\varvec{\eta }})\Big |_{t=0} = -{\varvec{\phi }}\,\llbracket {{\varvec{\eta }}},{{\varvec{\eta }}}\rrbracket _H\in T_{{\varvec{\phi }}}^{\perp }\,\text {St}(p,V). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}d2dt2Rϕpol(tη)|t=0=-ϕ〚η,η〛H∈Tϕ⊥St(p,V).Note that second-order retractions are advantageous for second-order Riemannian optimization methods; see, e.g. [1, Sect. 6.3].

The Grassmann Manifold

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {O}(p)$$\end{document}O(p) be the orthogonal group of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^{p\times p}$$\end{document}Rp×p. Following [47], we define the infinite-dimensional Grassmann manifold as the quotient\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {Gr}(p,V)= \text {St}(p,V)/\text {O}(p)\end{aligned}$$\end{document}Gr(p,V)=St(p,V)/O(p)of the Stiefel manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}St(p,V) with respect to the equivalence relation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\varvec{\phi }}\sim {\hat{{\varvec{\phi }}}} \qquad \Longleftrightarrow \qquad {\hat{{\varvec{\phi }}}} = {\varvec{\phi }}\, Q \; \text{ for } \text{ some } Q\in \text{ O }(p). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ∼ϕ^⟺ϕ^=ϕQforsomeQ∈O(p).The Grassmann manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Gr}(p,V)$$\end{document}Gr(p,V) can be interpreted as the set of the equivalence classes given by for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\phi }}\in \text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}ϕ∈St(p,V). Similarly to the Grassmann matrix manifold [1, Prop. 3.4.6], one can show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Gr}(p,V)$$\end{document}Gr(p,V) admits a unique structure of quotient manifold. A canonical projection from the Stiefel manifold into the Grassmann manifold is defined by and is a smooth submersion. This means that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{D}\,}}\! \pi ({\varvec{\phi }})$$\end{document}Dπ(ϕ) is surjective, and, hence, the equivalence class is an embedded submanifold of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}St(p,V); see [1, Prop. 3.4.4.].

In the following, we examine a useful connection of the Stiefel manifold and the Grassmann manifold. More precisely, we show that there is a one-to-one relation between the tangent space of the Grassmann manifold and the so-called horizontal space, a subspace of the tangent space of the Stiefel manifold. The tangent space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\phi }}\in \text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}ϕ∈St(p,V) defined in (14) can be splitted with respect to the projection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π and the metric g as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Remark 2

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=1$$\end{document}p=1, the Stiefel manifold coincides with the Grassmann manifold and equals the unit sphere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {S}}= \big \{ \phi \in {\tilde{V}}:\Vert \phi \Vert _{L^2(\Omega )} = 1 \big \}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S={ϕ∈V~:‖ϕ‖L2(Ω)=1}.Its tangent space is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\phi }\,{\mathbb {S}} = \{ \eta \in {\tilde{V}}: (\eta , \phi )_{L^2(\Omega )} = 0 \}$$\end{document}TϕS={η∈V~:(η,ϕ)L2(Ω)=0} and the orthogonal projection onto this space takes the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {P}}_{\phi }(y)=y-(\phi ,y)_{L^2(\Omega )}\phi $$\end{document}Pϕ(y)=y-(ϕ,y)L2(Ω)ϕ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in {\tilde{V}}$$\end{document}y∈V~. Furthermore, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\phi ,\eta )\in T{\mathbb {S}}$$\end{document}(ϕ,η)∈TS, the second-order retraction and the exponential mapping on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {S}}$$\end{document}S are given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {R}}(\phi ,\eta )&=\frac{\phi +\eta \qquad }{\Vert \phi +\eta \Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}}, \\ \textrm{Exp}(\phi ,\eta )&=\cos \Big (\Vert \eta \Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}\Big )\phi +\sin \Big (\Vert \eta \Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}\Big )\frac{\eta \qquad }{\Vert \eta \Vert _{L^2(\Omega )}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R(ϕ,η)=ϕ+η‖ϕ+η‖L2(Ω),Exp(ϕ,η)=cos(‖η‖L2(Ω))ϕ+sin(‖η‖L2(Ω))η‖η‖L2(Ω),respectively.

Riemannian Newton Methods

In this section, we present Riemannian Newton methods on the Stiefel manifold as well as on the Grassmann manifold and discuss the inexact version of the latter.

Within the Riemannian Newton method on the Stiefel manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}St(p,V), for given iterate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\eta }}_k\in T_{{\varvec{\phi }}_k} \text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}ηk∈TϕkSt(p,V) by solving the Newton equation 29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{Hess}\,}}{\mathcal {E}}({\varvec{\phi }}_k) It should, however, be noted that due to the non-uniqueness of the minimizer of (5) caused by the invariance of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {E}}$$\end{document}E under orthogonal transformations, we cannot expect that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{Hess}\,}}{\mathcal {E}}({\varvec{\phi }}_k)$$\end{document}HessE(ϕk) is invertible on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{{\varvec{\phi }}_k} \text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}TϕkSt(p,V). Theorem 2

It follows from Theorem 2 that if Eq. (29) is solvable, its solution is not unique. For solving the Newton equation (30), we can employ any matrix-free iterative linear solver which does not require the storage of the coefficient matrix explicitly but accesses it by computing the matrix–vector product or—as in our case—by evaluating the linear operator given in (28). The resulting Riemannian Newton method on the Grassmann manifold is presented in Algorithm 1. Algorithm 1Riemannian Newton method on the Grassmann manifold

Riemannian Newton method on the Grassmann manifold

One important property guaranteeing an isolated local minimum of the energy is that the Hessian is positive at a stationary point. Due to the assumed spectral gap, this yields all together which completes the proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 3

(Connection to the Lagrange–Newton method) The optimal solution of the constrained minimization problem (5) can also be determined by the Lagrange–Newton method. Based on the first-order optimality conditions () with a symmetric Lagrange multiplier, we aim to solve the nonlinear system of equations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f({\varvec{\phi }}, \Lambda ) = \begin{bmatrix} {\mathcal {J}}{\mathcal {A}}_{{\varvec{\phi }}} \,{\varvec{\phi }}-{\varvec{\phi }}\, \Lambda \\ \llbracket {{\varvec{\phi }}},{{\varvec{\phi }}}\rrbracket _H - I_p \\ \Lambda -\Lambda ^T\end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} {{\textbf {0}}}\, \\ 0_p \\ 0_p\end{bmatrix}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(ϕ,Λ)=JAϕϕ-ϕΛ〚ϕ,ϕ〛H-IpΛ-ΛT=00p0p.Computing the Jacobian of f, the Lagrange-Newton iteration is given as follows: for given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\phi }}_k\in V$$\end{document}ϕk∈V and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _k\in {\mathbb {R}}^{p\times p}$$\end{document}Λk∈Rp×p, solve the equations 33a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathcal {J}}{\mathcal {A}}_{{\varvec{\phi }}_k} {\varvec{\eta }}_k + {\mathcal {J}}\,{\mathcal {B}}_{{\varvec{\phi }}_k} {\varvec{\eta }}_k -{\varvec{\eta }}_k\Lambda _k-{\varvec{\phi }}_k\Xi _k&= -\big ({\mathcal {J}}{\mathcal {A}}_{{\varvec{\phi }}_k} {\varvec{\phi }}_k -{\varvec{\phi }}_k\Lambda _k\big ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}JAϕkηk+JBϕkηk-ηkΛk-ϕkΞk=-(JAϕkϕk-ϕkΛk),33b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \llbracket {{\varvec{\phi }}_k},{{\varvec{\eta }}_k}\rrbracket _H+\llbracket {{\varvec{\eta }}_k},{{\varvec{\phi }}_k}\rrbracket _H&= -\big (\llbracket {{\varvec{\phi }}_k},{{\varvec{\phi }}_k}\rrbracket _H - Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\phi }}_{k+1}$$\end{document}ϕk+1 does not necessarily belong to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {St}(p,V)$$\end{document}St(p,V). This shows that the Lagrange–Newton method with the modified update \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\varvec{\phi }}_{k+1} = {\mathcal {R}}({\varvec{\phi }}_k,{\varvec{\eta }}_k), \qquad \Lambda _{k+1} = \llbracket {{\varvec{\phi }}_{k+1}},{{\mathcal {J}}{\mathcal {A}}_{{\varvec{\phi }}_{k+1}}\,{\varvec{\phi }}_{k+1}}\rrbracket _H \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕk+1=R(ϕk,ηk),Λk+1=〚ϕk+1,JAϕk+1ϕk+1〛His equivalent to the Riemannian Newton method on the Stiefel manifold.

Examples and Numerical Experiments

This section is devoted to the numerical investigation of the Riemannian Newton methods. To this end, we consider the Gross–Pitaevskii eigenvalue problem from Example 1 and the Kohn–Sham model from Example 2.

Gross–Pitaevskii Eigenvalue Problem

The minimization of the Gross–Pitaevskii energy functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {E}}_{\textrm{GP}}$$\end{document}EGP in (6) leads to the following nonlinear eigenvector problem: find \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi \in {\tilde{V}} = H^1_0(\Omega )$$\end{document}ϕ∈V~=H01(Ω) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \phi \Vert _{L^2(\Omega )} = 1$$\end{document}‖ϕ‖L2(Ω)=1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}λ∈R such that 34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -\Delta \phi + 2\,\vartheta \, \phi + \kappa \, |\phi |^2 \phi = \lambda \, \phi \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-Δϕ+2ϑϕ+κ|ϕ|2ϕ=λϕfor some space-dependent external potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta \ge 0$$\end{document}ϑ≥0 and an interaction constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa >0$$\end{document}κ>0. The latter means that the particle interactions are repulsive, i.e., we consider the so-called defocussing regime. In this case, we get the operators 35a\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle {\mathcal {A}}_\phi \, v, w\rangle&= \int \limits _\Omega (\nabla v)^T \nabla w \,\text {d}x+ 2 \int \limits _\Omega \vartheta \, v w \,\text {d}x+ \kappa \int \limits _\Omega \, \phi ^2 v w \,\text {d}x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨Aϕv,w⟩=∫Ω(∇v)T∇wdx+2∫Ωϑvwdx+κ∫Ωϕ2vwdx,35b\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle {\mathcal {B}}_\phi \, v, w\rangle&= 2\kappa \int \limits _{\Omega } \phi ^2 v w \,\text {d}x \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨Bϕv,w⟩=2κ∫Ωϕ2vwdx for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v,w\in {\tilde{V}}$$\end{document}v,w∈V~. One can see that the bilinear form defined through (35a) corresponds to the Laplacian with the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-shift \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\vartheta +\kappa \phi ^2$$\end{document}2ϑ+κϕ2. Assuming this shift to be constant and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega =(0,1)^d$$\end{document}Ω=(0,1)d as the spatial domain, Assumption 3 is satisfied; see [50, Ch. 12]. Moreover, the nonlinear operator from (35b) fulfills \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big ( {\mathcal {J}}{\mathcal {B}}_{\phi }\, \psi , \psi \big )_{L^2(\Omega )} = \big \langle {\mathcal {B}}_{\phi }\,\psi , \psi \big \rangle = 2\kappa \int \limits _{\Omega } \phi ^2\, \psi ^2 \,\text {d}x\ge 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(JBϕψ,ψ)L2(Ω)=〈Bϕψ,ψ〉=2κ∫Ωϕ2ψ2dx≥0,such that Theorem 4 is applicable.

For the spatial discretization of the Gross–Pitaevskii problem (34), we use a biquadratic finite element method on a Cartesian mesh of width h; see [16] for the corresponding error analysis. The resulting discrete eigenvalue problem reads \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A \varphi + 2M_{\vartheta } \varphi + \kappa M_{\varphi ^2} \varphi = \lambda \, M \varphi , \qquad \varphi ^TM\varphi =1 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Aφ+2Mϑφ+κMφ2φ=λMφ,φTMφ=1with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in {\mathbb {R}}^n$$\end{document}φ∈Rn, where n denotes the number of degrees of freedom. Here, A is the stiffness matrix, M is the mass matrix, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\vartheta }$$\end{document}Mϑ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\varphi ^2}$$\end{document}Mφ2 are the weighted mass matrices, respectively, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ^2$$\end{document}φ2 should be understood as the elementwise product. Then the discrete version of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\llbracket {\phi },{{\mathcal {J}}\!{\mathcal {A}}_\phi \,\phi }\rrbracket _H=\big (\phi ,{\mathcal {J}}\!{\mathcal {A}}_\phi \,\phi \big )_{L^2(\Omega )}$$\end{document}〚ϕ,JAϕϕ〛H=(ϕ,JAϕϕ)L2(Ω) equals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _\varphi = \varphi ^T (A + 2M_{\vartheta } + \kappa M_{\varphi ^2})\, \varphi , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λφ=φT(A+2Mϑ+κMφ2)φ,and the Newton equation takes the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (I - M\varphi \varphi ^T)\big ((A + 2M_{\vartheta } + 3\kappa&M_{\varphi ^2})\, \psi - \lambda _\varphi M \psi \big ) \\&= - (I - M\varphi \varphi ^T)(A + 2M_{\vartheta } + \kappa M_{\varphi ^2})\, \varphi \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(I-MφφT)((A+2Mϑ+3κMφ2)ψ-λφMψ)=-(I-MφφT)(A+2Mϑ+κMφ2)φwith unknown \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi \in \{\xi \in {\mathbb {R}}^n\;:\; \xi ^TM\varphi = 0\}=\text {im}(I-\varphi \varphi ^T\! M)$$\end{document}ψ∈{ξ∈Rn:ξTMφ=0}=im(I-φφTM).

We demonstrate the performance of the resulting Riemannian Newton method in comparison with the SCF iteration combined with the optimal damping algorithm (ODA) proposed in [23] and the energy-adaptive Riemannian gradient descent method (RGD) of [29] with a non-monotone step size control as outlined in [8]. The numerical experiments are performed on a sufficiently large bounded domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega = (-L,L)^2$$\end{document}Ω=(-L,L)2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=8$$\end{document}L=8, for two types of trapping potentials. In Sect. 5.1.1, we consider a simple harmonic trap, whereas in Sect. 5.1.2, we add an additional disorder potential. The interaction parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ as well as the spatial resolution h will be specified separately for each case.

Ground State in a Harmonic Trap

For the first numerical experiment, we consider the harmonic trapping potential 36\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vartheta _\text {harm}(x) = \tfrac{1}{2}\Vert x\Vert ^2 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϑharm(x)=12‖x‖2and the interaction parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =10,100,1000$$\end{document}κ=10,100,1000. The resulting ground states computed on a Cartesian mesh of width \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h/(2\,L) = 2^{-10}$$\end{document}h/(2L)=2-10 are depicted in Fig. 1.Fig. 1Ground state in the harmonic trap (potential in gray, properly rescaled) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =10,100,1000$$\end{document}κ=10,100,1000 (from left to right)

Ground state in the harmonic trap (potential in gray, properly rescaled) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =10,100,1000$$\end{document}κ=10,100,1000 (from left to right)

To generate a joint and sufficiently accurate initial value for the three solvers of the discretized nonlinear eigenvector problem, we run the energy-adaptive RGD method starting from the biquadratic finite element interpolation of the constant 1 (respecting the homogeneous Dirichlet boundary condition). We stopped this iteration once the residual fell below the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-2}$$\end{document}10-2 tolerance and used the approximated ground state as the initial state to compare the asymptotic behavior of the three different solvers. The corresponding convergence histories are presented in Fig. 2 showing the evolution of the residuals during the iteration processes. It can be observed that the Riemannian Newton method (with sparse direct solution of the Newton equation using the Sherman–Morrison formula [48]) reaches the tolerance of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-8}$$\end{document}10-8 in only three steps. While the performances of the SCF iteration and the energy-adaptive RGD method abate with increasing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ, the Riemannian Newton scheme appears to be extremely robust. We would like to emphasize that, although one Newton step is slightly more expensive than one step of any other competing method, the overall costs are much smaller, especially for increasing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ.Fig. 2Convergence history of the residuals for the ground state in the harmonic trap for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =10,100,1000$$\end{document}κ=10,100,1000 (from left to right)

Convergence history of the residuals for the ground state in the harmonic trap for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =10,100,1000$$\end{document}κ=10,100,1000 (from left to right)

The convergence behavior of the Riemannian Newton scheme is also robust to the underlying mesh size h and, hence, independent of the dimension of the discretization space. This is demonstrated in Fig. 3 with a fixed choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =1000$$\end{document}κ=1000. We consider a sequence of meshes with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h/(2L) = 2^{-1},\ldots ,2^{-10}$$\end{document}h/(2L)=2-1,…,2-10 and use the same procedure as above to generate initial guesses with residuals of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-2}$$\end{document}10-2. The left graph shows the number of (outer) iterations of the Riemannian Newton method to fall below the tolerance of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-10}$$\end{document}10-10 for each of these mesh sizes. An increase in the number of iterations with smaller mesh size is not observed. In our experience, this mesh independence of the Riemannian Newton optimization scheme is representative for many other choices of potentials and interaction parameters. Note, however, that this does not mean that the costs of a Newton step are independent of h. As for every Laplace-type problem, methods such as multigrid need to be implemented in order to obtain a mesh-independence also for the inner iteration. This holds for all competing methods in the same way. For completeness, Fig. 3 also shows the corresponding errors in the minimum energy approximation as a function of the mesh size, demonstrating the optimal fourth-order convergence rate of the biquadratic finite element implementation [30].Fig. 3Computing the ground state in the harmonic trap for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =1000$$\end{document}κ=1000: iteration count of the Riemannian Newton method to fall below the tolerance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-10}$$\end{document}10-10 (left) and the error in the minimal energy (right) versus mesh size h. The dashed line indicates order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h^4$$\end{document}h4

Computing the ground state in the harmonic trap for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =1000$$\end{document}κ=1000: iteration count of the Riemannian Newton method to fall below the tolerance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-10}$$\end{document}10-10 (left) and the error in the minimal energy (right) versus mesh size h. The dashed line indicates order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h^4$$\end{document}h4

Localized Ground State in a Disorder Potential

The second experiment considers the computationally more difficult case where the external potential is the sum of the harmonic potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _\text {harm}$$\end{document}ϑharm defined in (36) and a potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _\text {rand}$$\end{document}ϑrand reflecting a high degree of disorder. The disorder part \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _\text {rand}$$\end{document}ϑrand is chosen as a piecewise constant function on the Cartesian mesh of width \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2L\varepsilon $$\end{document}2Lε, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon = 2^{-6}$$\end{document}ε=2-6, taking values 0 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon ^{-2}$$\end{document}ε-2 as depicted in Fig. 4. For a potential in such a scaling regime, the low-energy eigenstates essentially localize in terms of an exponential decay of their moduli relative to the small parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon $$\end{document}ε. For the linear case, i.e., for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =0$$\end{document}κ=0, this has been analyzed in [4]. For growing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ, the ground state consists of a growing number of localized peaks; see Fig. 4. Further details on the phenomenon of localization in the Gross–Pitaevskii equation and the onset of delocalization can be found in [6, 7]. As in the previous experiments, we use biquadratic finite elements on a Cartesian mesh of width \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h/(2L) = 2^{-10}$$\end{document}h/(2L)=2-10. To illustrate the localization behavior that occurs with the current parameter scaling for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \lesssim 1$$\end{document}κ≲1, we consider the interaction parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =0.1,1,10$$\end{document}κ=0.1,1,10. The ground states for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =1,10$$\end{document}κ=1,10 are shown in Fig. 4. The ground state for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =0.1$$\end{document}κ=0.1 is hardly distinguishable from the one for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =1$$\end{document}κ=1 and, therefore, it is not shown in a separate figure.Fig. 4Piecewise constant disorder potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _\text {rand}$$\end{document}ϑrand (left, black elements refer to the value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon ^{-2}$$\end{document}ε-2, white elements refer to the value 0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon =2^{-6}$$\end{document}ε=2-6) and the corresponding ground states for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =1$$\end{document}κ=1 (middle) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =10$$\end{document}κ=10 (right)

Piecewise constant disorder potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta _\text {rand}$$\end{document}ϑrand (left, black elements refer to the value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon ^{-2}$$\end{document}ε-2, white elements refer to the value 0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon =2^{-6}$$\end{document}ε=2-6) and the corresponding ground states for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =1$$\end{document}κ=1 (middle) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =10$$\end{document}κ=10 (right)

Figure 5 displays the convergence history of the residuals for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =0.1,1,10$$\end{document}κ=0.1,1,10. We employed the same strategy as in Sect. 5.1.1 to generate suitable initial guesses with the residuals of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-2}$$\end{document}10-2 used for all methods. The results clearly indicate that the ground state computations with the disorder potential are already challenging for smaller values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ. Particularly, the energy-adaptive RGD method needs much larger iteration counts, which according to [27], may be related to smaller spectral gaps between the first and second eigenvalue. The Riemannian Newton method, on the other hand, still performs well and reaches the prescribed tolerance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-8}$$\end{document}10-8 for the residual in only a few steps in all three examples. For comparison, the SCF iteration converges very fast in the almost linear case but suffers from larger values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ as the energy-adaptive RGD method. Here, again, the higher costs per Newton step are compensated by far by the very small number of needed iteration steps.Fig. 5Convergence history of the residuals for the ground state in a disorder potential for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa =0.1,1,10$$\end{document}κ=0.1,1,10 (from left to right)
