Kohn–Sham Model

For the Kohn–Sham energy functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {E}}_{\textrm{KS}}$$\end{document}EKS introduced in (7), we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle {\mathcal {A}}_{\varvec{\phi }}\,\varvec{v}, \varvec{w}\rangle&= \int \limits _\Omega {{\,\textrm{tr}\,}}\bigl ((\nabla \varvec{v})^T\nabla \varvec{w}\bigr ) \,\text {d}x+ 2 \int \limits _\Omega \vartheta _\text {ion}\, \varvec{v}\cdot \varvec{w}\,\text {d}x\\&\qquad + 2\int \limits _\Omega \Big (\int \limits _{\Omega } \frac{\rho ({\varvec{\phi }}(y))}{\Vert x-y\Vert }\, \,\text {d}y\Big )\, \varvec{v}\cdot \varvec{w}\,\text {d}x+ 2 \int \limits _\Omega \mu _{\textrm{xc}}(\rho ({\varvec{\phi }}))\, \varvec{v}\cdot \varvec{w}\,\text {d}x\end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨Aϕv,w⟩=∫Ωtr((∇v)T∇w)dx+2∫Ωϑionv·wdx+2∫Ω(∫Ωρ(ϕ(y))‖x-y‖dy)v·wdx+2∫Ωμxc(ρ(ϕ))v·wdxwith \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _{\textrm{xc}}(\rho ) = \frac{{\textrm{d}}}{{{\textrm{d}}}\rho } \big (\rho \,\epsilon _{\textrm{xc}}(\rho )\big )$$\end{document}μxc(ρ)=ddρ(ρϵxc(ρ)). Moreover, the operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {B}}_{\varvec{\phi }}$$\end{document}Bϕ has the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle {\mathcal {B}}_{\varvec{\phi }}\,\varvec{v},\varvec{w}\rangle = 4 \int \limits _{\Omega }\!\Big (\!\int \limits _{\Omega } \frac{{\varvec{\phi }}\cdot \varvec{v}}{\Vert x-y\Vert } \,\text {d}y\Big ) \,{\varvec{\phi }}\cdot \varvec{w}\,\text {d}x+ 4 \int \limits _{\Omega } \zeta _\textrm{xc}(\rho ({\varvec{\phi }})) ({\varvec{\phi }}\cdot \varvec{v})\, ({\varvec{\phi }}\cdot \varvec{w}) \,\text {d}x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨Bϕv,w⟩=4∫Ω(∫Ωϕ·v‖x-y‖dy)ϕ·wdx+4∫Ωζxc(ρ(ϕ))(ϕ·v)(ϕ·w)dx,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _{\textrm{xc}}(\rho )=\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}\rho } \mu _\textrm{xc}(\rho )$$\end{document}ζxc(ρ)=ddρμxc(ρ). The exchange-correlation function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _\text {xc}(\rho )$$\end{document}ϵxc(ρ) can additively be decomposed as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _\text {xc}(\rho ) =\epsilon _\text {x}(\rho ) + \epsilon _\text {c}(\rho )$$\end{document}ϵxc(ρ)=ϵx(ρ)+ϵc(ρ), where the exchange component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _\text {x}(\rho )$$\end{document}ϵx(ρ) has the particular analytical expression \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _\text {x}(\rho )=-\frac{3}{4}\big (\frac{3}{\pi }\rho \big )^{1/3}$$\end{document}ϵx(ρ)=-34(3πρ)1/3 and the correlation component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _\text {c}(\rho )$$\end{document}ϵc(ρ) is usually unknown, but can be fitted by using quantum Monte-Carlo data [41]. For the numerical experiments, we use the MATLAB toolbox KSSOLV [35, 51], in which the correlation component is implemented as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \epsilon _\text {c}(\rho ) = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} a_1 + a_2\,r(\rho ) + \big (a_3 + a_4\,r(\rho )\big ) \ln (r(\rho )), &{}\text {if } r(\rho )<1, \\[0.3em] \big ( 1+b_2\,\sqrt{r(\rho )}+b_3\,r(\rho ) \big )/b_1, &{}\text {if } r(\rho )\ge 1,\end{array}\right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϵc(ρ)=a1+a2r(ρ)+(a3+a4r(ρ))ln(r(ρ)),ifr(ρ)<1,[0.3em](1+b2r(ρ)+b3r(ρ))/b1,ifr(ρ)≥1,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r(\rho )=\big (\frac{4\pi }{3}\rho \big )^{-1/3}$$\end{document}r(ρ)=(4π3ρ)-1/3 is the Wigner-Seitz radius, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_j,b_j\in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}aj,bj∈R are fitted constants; see [42, App. C].

For the spatial discretization, we employ the planewave discretization method as implemented in KSSOLV. &{} 0, \end{array} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A(Φ)Φ-ΦΛ=0,Φ∗Φ-Ip=0,where the discrete Kohn–Sham Hamiltonian is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A(\Phi ) = L + 2\, D_{\textrm{ion}} + 2{{\,\textrm{Diag}\,}}\big (L^+\rho _h(\Phi ) + \mu _{\textrm{xc}}(\rho _h(\Phi ))\big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A(Φ)=L+2Dion+2Diag(L+ρh(Φ)+μxc(ρh(Φ))).Further, the Riemannian gradient of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E(\Phi )$$\end{document}E(Φ) becomes 37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}E(\Phi ) = (I-\Phi \Phi ^*)A(\Phi )\Phi = A(\Phi )\Phi -\Phi \big (\Phi ^*\!A(\Phi )\Phi \big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}gradE(Φ)=(I-ΦΦ∗)A(Φ)Φ=A(Φ)Φ-Φ(Φ∗A(Φ)Φ).In the Riemannian Newton method on the Stiefel manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {St}(p,n)$$\end{document}St(p,n), we need to solve the equation 38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_{\Phi }\big (A(\Phi )\Psi +B(\Phi ,\Psi )-\Psi \Phi ^*\!A(\Phi )\Phi \big ) = -(I-\Phi \Phi ^*) A(\Phi )\Phi \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PΦ(A(Φ)Ψ+B(Φ,Ψ)-ΨΦ∗A(Φ)Φ)=-(I-ΦΦ∗)A(Φ)Φfor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi $$\end{document}Ψ belonging to the tangent space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_\Phi \, \text {St}(p,n)$$\end{document}TΦSt(p,n). Therein, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_{\Phi }(Y) = Y - \tfrac{1}{2}\, \Phi \big (\Phi ^* Y + Y^* \Phi \big ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PΦ(Y)=Y-12Φ(Φ∗Y+Y∗Φ)is the orthogonal projector onto \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_\Phi \,\text {St}(p,n)$$\end{document}TΦSt(p,n) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} B(\Phi ,\Psi ) = 2{{\,\textrm{Diag}\,}}\big ((L^++{{\,\textrm{Diag}\,}}(\zeta _\textrm{xc}(\rho _h(\Phi )))){{\,\textrm{diag}\,}}(\Phi \Psi ^*+\Psi \Phi ^*)\big )\Phi \end{aligned}$$\end{document}B(Φ,Ψ)=2Diag((L++Diag(ζxc(ρh(Φ))))diag(ΦΨ∗+ΨΦ∗))Φis the discretization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {B}}_{\varvec{\phi }}$$\end{document}Bϕ. On the Grassmann manifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Gr}(p,n)=\text {St}(p,n)/\text {U}(p)$$\end{document}Gr(p,n)=St(p,n)/U(p) with the unitary group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {U}(p)$$\end{document}U(p), the Newton equation takes the form 39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (I-\Phi \Phi ^*) \big (A(\Phi )\Psi _{\Phi }^\textrm{h} + B(\Phi ,\Psi _{\Phi }^\textrm{h})-\Psi _{\Phi }^\textrm{h}\Phi ^*\!A(\Phi )\Phi \big ) = -(I-\Phi \Phi ^*) A(\Phi )\Phi \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(I-ΦΦ∗)(A(Φ)ΨΦh+B(Φ,ΨΦh)-ΨΦhΦ∗A(Φ)Φ)=-(I-ΦΦ∗)A(Φ)Φfor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _\Phi ^\textrm{h}\in {\mathcal {H}}_{\Phi } = \{\Psi \in T_\Phi \, \text {St}(p,n)\;:\; \Phi ^*\Psi =0\}$$\end{document}ΨΦh∈HΦ={Ψ∈TΦSt(p,n):Φ∗Ψ=0}.

In our experiments, we compare the calculation of the ground state by using the SCF iteration with the Anderson charge mixing scheme [51], the energy-adaptive RDG with non-monotone step size control [8], and the Riemannian Newton methods on the Stiefel manifold (RNS) and on the Grassmann manifold (RNG). In all these methods, we choose the same initial guess for the wave function by performing one SCF step with a randomly generated starting point and stop the iterations once the Frobenius norm of the residual \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R(\Phi _k) = A(\Phi _k)\Phi _k-\Phi _k\Lambda _k$$\end{document}R(Φk)=A(Φk)Φk-ΦkΛk with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _k=\Phi _k^*A(\Phi _k)\Phi _k$$\end{document}Λk=Φk∗A(Φk)Φk is smaller than the tolerance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-8}$$\end{document}10-8. Note that due to (37), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert R(\Phi _k)\Vert _F=\Vert {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}E(\Phi _k)\Vert _F$$\end{document}‖R(Φk)‖F=‖gradE(Φk)‖F, i.e., the norms of the residuals provide the information on the size of the Riemannian gradients. In both Newton methods and the energy-adaptive RGD method, we use the qR decomposition based retractions. The reference minimal energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\min }$$\end{document}Emin is computed by the RNG method with the tolerance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-10}$$\end{document}10-10.

All algorithms are performed in an inexact manner, i.e., the occurring linear systems are only solved up to a certain tolerance. In RNG, for instance, we follow Algorithm 1 using the MATLAB built-in function minres as a linear system solver with the adaptive tolerance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\min (1/k, 10^{-3} \Vert {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}E(\Phi _{k-1})\Vert _F)$$\end{document}min(1/k,10-3‖gradE(Φk-1)‖F) and the maximal number of inner iterations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _{\max }=15$$\end{document}ℓmax=15. The remaining parameters are chosen as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta =10^{-8}$$\end{document}η=10-8, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta =0.5$$\end{document}δ=0.5, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma =10^{-4}$$\end{document}σ=10-4. In RNS, we proceed similarly, with the only difference that instead of (39) we solve the Newton equation of the form (38). For solving the linear eigenvalue problems in SCF, we employ the KSSOLV built-in LOBPCG algorithm for the pentacene model in Sect. 5.2.1 and the MATLAB built-in function eigs for the graphene model in Sect. 5.2.2. Switching to another eigenvalue solver is necessary due to the ill-conditioning in LOBPCG for the latter example. In both cases, the tolerance for the inner iterations is set to be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\min (10^{-3}, 10^{-3} \Vert {{\,\textrm{grad}\,}}E(\Phi _{k-1})\Vert _F)$$\end{document}min(10-3,10-3‖gradE(Φk-1)‖F). For the linear system solvers, we use the kinetic energy preconditioner, which provides, especially for the energy-adaptive RGD method, better numerical results than the KSSOLV built-in Teter–Payne–Allan preconditioner [49].

Finally, we would like to mention that similarly to the Gross–Pitaevskii example in Sect. 5.1, the Riemannian Newton schemes for the Kohn–Sham model are again robust in terms of the dimension of the discretization space used in KSSOLV. This means that the number of Newton iterations needed to fall below a certain tolerance is not effected by finer discretizations (as long as the number of inner iterations is sufficiently large).

Pentacene Molecule

In the first numerical experiment, we calculate the ground state for the pentacene molecule \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{C}_{22}\textrm{H}_{14}$$\end{document}C22H14 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=51$$\end{document}p=51 electron orbitals. A spatial planewave discretization on a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$80\times \ 55\times 160$$\end{document}80×55×160 sampling grid gives the discrete model of dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=44791$$\end{document}n=44791. In Fig. 6, we present the convergence history of the residuals and the energy reduction during the iterations. One can see that the RNS and RNG methods have very similar behavior and converge within 8 and 10 iterations, respectively. In comparison, the SCF method and the energy-adaptive RGD method require 18 and 54 iterations to converge, respectively. In terms of computing time, all methods perform quite similarly in this experiment. This can also be seen in Table 1, which shows the values of the energy functional, the reached residuals, the number of (outer) iterations, the total number of Hamiltonian evaluations, and the CPU Numerical results for the pentacene and the graphene models

Graphene Lattice

As the second model, we consider a graphene lattice consisting of carbon atoms arranged in 9 hexagons with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=67$$\end{document}p=67 electron orbitals. We use a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$32\times \ 55\times 160$$\end{document}32×55×160 sampling grid for the wave function and get a discrete model of dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=12279$$\end{document}n=12279. Figure 7 presents the evolution of the residuals and errors in the energy. We observe again that both Newton methods converge very fast compared to the energy-adaptive RGD method which needs 64 iterations to achieve the tolerance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10^{-8}$$\end{document}10-8 for the residual. In contrary, the SCF iteration has difficulties to converge. Also other mixing strategies implemented in KSSOLV do not improve the convergence property of SCF for the graphene model. This behaviour may be explained by a missing spectral gap between the excited and non-excited states. A detailed comparison, including the overall CPU time is part of Table 1. In this experiment (with the particular implementation and used hardware), one can say that the computational complexity of the methods follows the rule of thumb \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {1 step Newton} \quad \approx \quad \text {2 steps SCF} \quad \approx \quad \text {4 steps eaRGD}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}1 step Newton≈2 steps SCF≈4 steps eaRGD.Overall, this example clearly shows the supremacy of the Newton approach for more challenging examples.

Conclusion

In this paper, we have derived Riemannian Newton methods on the infinite-dimensional Stiefel and Grassmann manifolds for Kohn–Sham type energy minimization problems. Starting from an energy functional, we present a unified approach for applications in computational physics (e.g., the Gross–Pitaevskii eigenvalue problem) and computational chemistry (e.g., the Kohn–Sham model). The remarkable gain in computational efficiency of the Riemannian Newton methods compared to the so far more popular methods such as SCF and gradient descent methods is demonstrated by a series of numerical experiments.
